Liver rows other than the multi-mapping fraction are exploratory (not pre-specified).

3.4 Robustness to GC content and sequencing depth

Across breast samples, the multi-mapping fraction was correlated with sequencing depth (Spearman ρ = -0.68) and with the GC slope (ρ = 0.49), and the heterochromatic share less so (ρ = -0.52 and 0.30). Depth did not differ between cases and controls (above). Adjusting for these covariates within each training fold reduced but did not remove the signal (Fig. 2a). The multimapping fraction gave LOPO AUC 0.64 after adjustment for the GC slope, 0.76 after adjustment for depth and 0.76 after adjustment for both. The heterochromatic share was less affected (0.71, 0.76 and 0.76). With fragment-level GC weighting, the multi-mapping fraction remained higher in cancer (median 5.34 % vs 5.16 %; 18/25 pairs; p = 0.003; LOPO AUC 0.78).

3.5 Relation to tumour-burden proxies

The multi-mapping signal did not increase with disease extent. The case-control difference in the multi-mapping fraction was largest in stage I (median 0.36 percentage points), smaller in stage II (0.17) and close to zero in stage III (0.02; Spearman ρ with stage -0.42, p = 0.04). The difference in the heterochromatic share showed no trend with stage (median 1.23, 0.70 and 0.86 percentage points; ρ = -0.23, p = 0.27). Neither difference was associated with grade (ρ = -0.01 and 0.10; 24 pairs with grade recorded), and neither feature was associated with the copy-number deviation score in cases (ρ = 0.23, p = 0.27; ρ = 0.16, p = 0.44). These data therefore do not show that the signal scales with tumour burden; with 5 stage III pairs and no direct tumour-fraction measurement, they also cannot exclude it.

3.6 Comparison with public tissue methylation maps

Breast tumours were hypomethylated relative to the adjacent normal sample in every compartment (Table S5). The loss was similar in satellites and in the common-PMD positive control. Mean tumour -normal differences were -0.081 in alpha satellite (range across tumours -0.157 to +0.009), -0.115 in other satellites (-0.163 to -0.026), -0.101 in the remaining heterochromatic bands and -0.113 in common PMDs, compared with -0.050 in common HMDs. Satellites were well methylated in normal breast (alpha 0.58, other satellites 0.76) and normal liver (0.63 and 0.70), so they have methylation to lose. WGBS covered only about 32,000 alphasatellite CpGs per sample, compared with about 9 million in common PMDs, because satellites map poorly with short reads.

In breast cfDNA, the increase in the heterochromatic share came from non-alpha satellites (share of low-MAPQ fragments 17.4 % vs 16.2 %; 18/25 pairs; p = 0.022), not from alpha satellite (15.3 % vs 14.7 %; 15/25; p = 0.21) or the remaining heterochromatic bands (6/25). The low-MAPQ fraction was also higher in cases inside common PMDs (3.69 % vs 3.56 %; 20/25; p = 0.002) and common HMDs (2.44 % vs 2.41 %; 21/25; p = 0.0005), so part of the excess is genome-wide. In liver cfDNA the pattern differed: the low-MAPQ fraction within alpha satellite was higher in 7/8 pairs (p = 0.035), whereas the non-alpha satellite share was lower in 5/8; the low-MAPQ fraction within common PMDs was higher in 8/8 pairs (p = 0.004).

The pre-specified bin-level test was in the predicted direction but weak: across 2,733 bins, the cfDNA excess was larger where breast tumours lost more methylation (Spearman ρ = -0.056, p = 0.004; within-chromosome circular-shift p = 0.006). The result was similar after excluding satellite-and heterochromatin-dominated bins (ρ = -0.050, p = 0.009). In the post hoc check, however, both quantities depended on bin GC content (cfDNA excess ρ = -0.39; methylation difference ρ = 0.14). After adjustment for GC, the association disappeared (partial ρ = 0.000, p = 0.99). The public maps are therefore compatible with satellite hypomethylation in breast tumours. They do not show a GC-independent link between where tumours lose methylation and where the cfDNA excess occurs.

3.7 Exploratory analysis in early-stage liver cancer

The pre-specified liver endpoint was not met. The multi-mapping fraction pointed in the same direction but did not differ significantly (5.43 % vs 5.28 %; 5/8 pairs; p = 0.20), and the breasttrained model M2 gave an AUC of 0.73 (95 % CI 0.36-1.00; 5/8 pairs; sign test p = 0.36), with sensitivity 0.88 and specificity 0.63 at the breast-derived threshold (Fig. 2c, Table 3).

In an exploratory analysis that was not pre-specified, the heterochromatic share was higher in the HCC case than in its matched control in 7/8 pairs (23.4 % vs 22.7 %; p = 0.016; Fig. 1d), and a breast-trained, locked model on this feature alone gave AUC 0.86 (95 % CI 0.62-1.00; 7/8 pairs; one-sided sign test p = 0.035). The heterochromatic share was not correlated with depth (ρ = -0.04) or the GC slope (ρ = 0.19) in the liver cohort. Across all 33 matched pairs, the cancer case had the higher heterochromatic share in 26 (post hoc one-sided sign test p = 0.0007) and the higher multi-mapping fraction in 24 (p = 0.007). Because these counts include the 25 discovery pairs, they summarise consistency of direction and are not independent confirmation. With 8 pairs, the liver result generates a hypothesis for a pre-specified test in a new cohort.

Table 3. Breast-trained models applied unchanged to liver cancer (8 HCC / 8 matched controls). Only M2 was pre-specified. Model AUC (95 % CI) Pairs case > control Sign test p M2 multi-mapping fraction (pre-specified) 0.73 (0.36-1.00) 5/8 0.36 Heterochromatic share (exploratory) 0.86 (0.62-1.00) 7/8 0.035 Multi-mapping + heterochromatic share (exploratory) 0.83 (0.50-1.00) 5/8 0.36

3.8 Conventional fragmentomic features do not survive GC control

Without fragment-level GC control (analysis i), conventional fragmentomic features appeared to separate the groups. The 11-feature model gave a breast LOPO AUC of 0.71 (95 % CI 0.56-0.86; permutation p = 0.019), the end-motif family 0.76 and the window features 0.68, and the locked 11-feature model gave AUC 0.77 in HCC (8/8 pairs). The fraction of C-starting ends (18/25 pairs; p = 0.003), FDI (18/25; p = 0.004) and IFS (18/25; p = 0.014) were higher in cancer (Figs. S2, S3). These features, however, tracked the per-sample coverage-GC dependence, which was stronger in the cancer case in 21/25 breast and 8/8 liver pairs (Spearman ρ with the GC slope 0.54-0.83 for the C-end fraction, 0.78-0.79 for FDI and 0.71-0.82 for IFS; Fig. S1).

Under each of the three GC-controlled analyses, the conventional models fell to chance (Table 4; Fig. 2b). With fragment-level GC weighting, no feature differed between cases and controls (all q > 0.7), and the 11-feature model gave AUC 0.36 in breast (permutation p = 0.84) and 0.44 in HCC. The GC-internal relative features were not correlated with the GC slope (|ρ| ≤ 0.32) and carried no signal at 1.4 M fragments (breast AUC 0.19, permutation p = 0.99; HCC 0.53) or at 10 M fragments (breast 0.48, permutation p = 0.52; HCC 0.61). At both depths, the uncorrected differences reappeared to the same extent in the GC-matched references: the C fraction at interior fragment positions was higher in the case in 22/25 breast and 8/8 liver pairs, and IFS and FDI at GC-matched non-TSS windows in 18-20/25 breast and 8/8 liver pairs. AUCs below 0.5 are expected under leave-one-pair-out cross-validation when features carry no signal.

Table 4. Conventional fragmentomic models under four GC-handling analyses (AUC; breast LOPO / liver locked). Analysis 11 features (or 5 relative) End motif Window (i) Bin-level GC correction only 0.71 / 0.77 0.76 / 0.84 0.68 / 0.72 (ii) Fragment-level GC weighting 0.36 / 0.44 0.36 / 0.31 0.34 / 0.47 (iii) GC-internal relative, 1.4 M 0.19 / 0.53 0.34 / 0.56 0.23 / 0.41 (iv) GC-internal relative, 10 M (19 + 8 pairs) 0.48 / 0.61 0.52 / 0.52 0.44 / 0.83* Multi-mapping fraction, for reference 0.81 / 0.73 --One of about 20 secondary model results across analyses (iii) and (iv); the same model gave 0.44 in breast

4. Discussion

In batch-matched early-stage breast cancer, the fraction of cfDNA fragments with low mapping quality was higher in cancer than in matched controls, and the excess was concentrated in centromeric and heterochromatic DNA. The signal was present at stage I, in every sequencing batch, after adjustment for GC content and depth, and under fragment-level GC weighting. The pre-specified liver endpoint, the breast-trained multi-mapping model, was not met. In an exploratory liver analysis, the share of multi-mapping fragments falling in centromeric and heterochromatic bands was higher in 7 of 8 pairs; this is a hypothesis for a pre-specified test in an independent cohort, not a replication. Conventional fragmentomic features, by contrast, separated the groups only when fragment GC composition was left uncontrolled.

Possible biological origin.

The localisation of the excess suggests, but does not establish, a contribution from repetitive pericentromeric DNA. Pericentromeric satellites are hypomethylated in many cancers, and this has been linked to chromatin decondensation and satellite transcription [15]; genome-wide repeat profiling of cfDNA has shown that repeat landscapes differ between cancer patients and healthy individuals [13]. One hypothesis consistent with this literature is that decondensed satellite chromatin is released or protected differently during cell death, increasing the representation of satellite-derived fragments in plasma. We did not measure methylation or chromatin accessibility in these samples, and three observations argue for caution. First, the multi-mapping difference was largest in stage I and smallest in stage III, and neither feature was associated with copy-number deviation, which is the opposite of what a purely tumour-derived signal would be expected to show as tumour burden rises. The signal may instead reflect non-tumour sources, such as a systemic or immune response to early disease, or pre-analytical factors. Second, the uniquely mapped (MAPQ ≥ 30) fragments also shifted slightly towards heterochromatic bands, which is consistent with a change in genomic origin, but the effect was small (15/25 pairs). Third, public tissue methylomes gave only indirect and weak support. Satellites were hypomethylated in breast tumours, but no more than common PMDs. The breast and liver excesses involved different satellite families, and the bin-level association between tumour hypomethylation and the cfDNA excess was explained by GC content. These tissue maps come from different patients and cover few satellite CpGs, so they cannot test the hypothesis directly. A direct test requires methylation-aware cfDNA data (bisulfite or enzymatic methylation sequencing) from the same samples.

Technical alternatives.

Mapping quality depends on the genomic origin of a fragment but also on read-level properties such as base quality, sequencing errors, read length and residual adapter sequence. The open data release used here contains aligned fragment coordinates and mapping quality but not reads or base qualities, so these factors could not be measured. Two observations from the fragment files are relevant but not decisive. The excess was distributed proportionally between MAPQ 0 fragments, which typically indicate equally good alternative alignments, and MAPQ 1-29 fragments, which also arise from sequencing errors in nearrepetitive sequence, so the MAPQ classes do not discriminate between the two explanations. The multi-mapping fraction is also almost the complement of the overall MAPQ ≥ 30 fraction (ρ = -0.97) and decreased with sequencing depth. The heterochromatic share is less exposed to a uniform read-quality effect, because it compares fragments within the multi-mapping set, and it was unrelated to depth and GC slope in the liver cohort. A definitive test requires re-alignment of raw reads with per-base Phred scores, mismatch rates, adapter-trimming statistics and readlength distributions compared between cases and controls.

Does GC control remove real biology?

Fragment-level GC weighting equalises each sample's fragment GC distribution to a genome-wide expectation. If cancer-derived cfDNA genuinely differs in GC composition, or if promoter-and CpG-island-rich regions carry a real fragmentation signal, part of that signal would be removed, so this correction alone could over-correct. For this reason we also used GC-internal features, which compare TSSs with non-promoter windows of the same GC content inside the same sample and therefore preserve promoter-specific differences. These features were also null, and the uncorrected case-control differences appeared to the same extent at GC-matched non-promoter windows and at interior fragment positions as at TSSs and fragment ends. The shift that drove the conventional features is therefore sample-wide rather than promoter-or cleavage-specific. A sample-wide GC shift could itself have a biological component, but it would not be specific to end motifs or TSS fragmentation. The multi-mapping features are not immune to GC: the multi-mapping fraction lost part of its performance after adjustment for the GC slope (LOPO AUC 0.81 to 0.64), whereas the heterochromatic share was less affected (0.76 to 0.71). We recommend that fragmentomic studies report the per-sample GC slope, test it between groups, and evaluate candidate features with both fragment-level and GC-matched internal controls.

Sample size and analysis history.

The cohorts are small (25 and 8 pairs). The multi-mapping fraction was discovered in the breast cohort, so the breast estimates are likely optimistic. The pooled 33-pair sign tests include the discovery pairs and were defined post hoc. Several GChandling analyses of the conventional features were run, and all are reported (Table 4; Supplementary Note 2).

Next steps.

A pre-specified test of the multi-mapping fraction and the heterochromatic share in larger, independent matched cohorts across cancer types, with raw reads and base-quality metrics; repeat-resolved quantification (satellite families, alpha-satellite higher-order repeats) with repeat-aware alignment; methylation-aware sequencing of the same cfDNA, with long-read or repeat-aware methods to resolve satellite methylation; and calibration against tumour fraction from deeper sequencing or tumour-informed assays.

Minimal residual disease.

These data do not address minimal residual disease (MRD) detection. The cohort contains only pre-treatment samples, with no serial sampling, recurrence outcomes or tumour-fraction estimates. The multi-mapping signal did not increase with stage or copynumber deviation, which an MRD marker would need to do. Because MRD assays compare a patient with their own baseline, within-person serial sampling would remove the betweenperson variation that limits this small-effect marker. Its relevance to MRD could therefore be tested in pre-and post-treatment samples from the same patients, with depth-matched sequencing, tumour-informed ctDNA measurements as reference and follow-up for recurrence. Until then, no MRD application should be inferred.

Limitations and proof-of-concept status.

This study represents a proof-of-concept discovery rather than a ready-to-use clinical test. The cohorts are relatively small (n=25 breast pairs and n=8 liver pairs) and rely on the retrospective analysis of public data. Because the multi-mapping fraction was discovered in the breast cohort, the performance estimates in breast cancer are likely optimistic, and the pooled 33-pair sign tests were defined post hoc. Furthermore, the public data release provided aligned fragment coordinates but lacked raw sequencing reads and per-base quality scores. Consequently, we cannot completely exclude the possibility that subtle machine read errors or pre-analytical technical factors contribute to the signal. Several GChandling analyses of the conventional features were run, and all are reported here to ensure transparency (Table 4) . Establishing the true clinical utility and biological origin of this marker will require prospective validation in larger, independent patient cohorts using raw sequence data.

5. Conclusion

By repurposing routinely discarded low-mapping-quality cfDNA fragments (MAPQ < 30), we demonstrate that multi-mapping reads from centromeric and heterochromatic regions are significantly elevated in early-stage breast cancer. Crucially, this robust marker survives rigorous GC-bias corrections that reduce conventional fragmentomic features (such as lengths and motifs) to random chance, exposing a major vulnerability in standard liquid biopsy pipelines. Unconventionally, the multi-mapping excess peaks in Stage I disease and does not scale with tumor burden, suggesting it may reflect an early systemic response rather than direct tumor shedding. Extracted at zero additional cost from standard whole-genome sequencing, this GCresistant marker challenges current filtering practices. While its exact biological origin requires confirmation and the liver cancer endpoint remains exploratory, it offers a highly accessible candidate for early cancer detection that warrants validation in larger independent cohorts. The four analyses in Table 4 were run in the order listed.
